Total return over each episode. The best sector in each row is marked in blue, the worst in plum. Sectors covering less than ninety per cent of an episode are excluded from the best and worst marks: real estate lists in 2015 and communications in 2018, so ranking their partial histories against funds that lived through the whole period would compare different spans under one label.

The policy regimes, and what led in each

Rates do not merely change; they sit in states, and the states last for years. Classifying every day since 1999 by where the funds rate stood against twelve months earlier produces nine spells of six months or more: three tightenings, three easings, two long stretches at the floor, and one period on hold. The floor alone accounts for ten years of the twenty-six — ninety-seven months after 2008 and twenty-six more after March 2020.

This is the regime structure an investor actually lived through, and it is coarser than the commentary suggests. There have been three tightening cycles in twenty-six years. Any claim about how sectors behave when the Fed raises rates rests on three observations.

RegimeFromLength Funds rateBest sectorWorst sector
Easing2001-0318m5.14→1.72Health care -1%Technology -46%
On hold2003-119m1.00→1.50Energy +31%Technology -8%
Tightening2004-0930m1.57→5.25Energy +88%Health care +17%
Easing2007-1012m4.60→0.36Staples -15%Financials -55%
At the floor2008-1297m0.47→0.66Discretionary +384%Energy +94%
Tightening2017-0426m0.83→2.36Technology +43%Energy -4%
At the floor2020-0326m0.25→0.83Energy +227%Utilities +51%
Tightening2022-0524m0.83→5.33Technology +45%Real estate -12%
Easing2024-0923m4.83→3.63Technology +73%Real estate +8%

A regime is labelled by the change in the effective funds rate over the preceding twelve months: a half point or more in either direction, or below half a per cent for the floor. Sector returns are total return, and a sector is shown only where its fund covered at least ninety per cent of the spell. The thresholds are chosen with hindsight and describe the record rather than a rule anyone could have applied on the first day of a regime.

The table's most useful feature is its inconsistency. Technology was the worst sector of the 2001 easing, losing 46%, and the best of both subsequent tightenings, gaining 43% and 45%. Energy was the best sector of a tightening cycle and also the best of a period pinned at the floor, two conditions with nothing in common. No sector leads two regimes of the same kind and no sector trails two.

Set against the earlier finding this makes sense. The rate sensitivities measured in that section were same-day repricings of a few tenths of a per cent. A tightening cycle lasts two years and contains an economy, an earnings season, a technology cycle and whatever else occurs. The discount-rate effect is real and is swamped over any horizon an investor actually holds for.

Not even the strongest case survives the table. Real estate carries the largest negative rate coefficient of any sector, at −4.89 per percentage point, and it duly finished worst of the 2022–24 tightening, falling 12% while the market rose. But it lived through two tightenings, and in the other — 2017 to 2019 — it gained 27% and energy finished worst instead.

So the sector with the cleanest measured sensitivity to rates got the direction right in one of the two cycles available to test it. That is what a real mechanism looks like when it is swamped by everything else: present in the arithmetic, unreliable in the outcome. The reader should be suspicious of anyone building a rotation on it — the sample that would support the claim is small enough to print in full, and has been, immediately above.

What the market was willing to pay

Valuation over the period did not oscillate around a mean so much as visit two extremes and spend most of its time nearer the higher one. On a cyclically adjusted basis — real price against a ten-year mean of real earnings — the market began the century at 43.2, a level without precedent in the record and not approached since. It fell to 22.0 by the 2002 trough, recovered to 27.3 before the 2007 peak, and reached 13.3 in March 2009. That last figure is the only genuinely cheap reading in the period.

Since then the direction has been one way. The pandemic low of 24.8 sat below the 2007 peak of 27.3 and comfortably inside the 13.3-to-32.1 range the ratio had covered between 2002 and 2017 — a fall, but not to anything a long-run investor would call cheap. By December 2021 the ratio stood at 38.3, and it has since ranged between 27 and 39 — a band that in 1999 would have been called a bubble and is now simply the weather. The investor who resolved in 2013 to wait for cheapness has now waited more than a decade, and the discipline that protected him in 2000 has cost him the entire subsequent advance.

The economy underneath

The real economy grew steadily and unspectacularly throughout, which is worth stating because the market's behaviour bore so little resemblance to it. Real output rose 75% across the period. Consumer prices rose 97%, so that a dollar of January 2000 purchases 51 cents today. Unemployment began at 4.0% and stands at 4.1% — a symmetry that conceals a journey to 14.8% in April 2020 and down to 3.4% in April 2023, the lowest reading in half a century.

Set the two records side by side and the disproportion is the lesson. Output grew by three-quarters; the total-return index multiplied by nearly nine. The excess is not production. It is some combination of profit margins, the price the market is willing to pay for a dollar of earnings, and the long decline in interest rates that ended in 2022. Only the first of those is properly the investor's, and none of the three is his to forecast.

The rate cycles, and the four inversions

The clearest macroeconomic signal available to an investor over this period announced itself four times in three clusters, and the record of what followed is neither as reliable nor as useless as either camp maintains. The ten-year Treasury yield fell below the two-year twice during 2000, once from October 2006 to March 2007, and most recently between July 2022 and September 2024 — the longest episode in this sample at twenty-six months, though the record here begins only in 2000. The first two clusters preceded the two great declines of the era. The third preceded nothing: equities rose strongly throughout it.

That is the whole sample. Three clusters, two of them followed by disaster and one by a boom, and the investor is invited to draw conclusions. He should decline. A signal with three observations is not a signal; it is an anecdote with a chart. The deepest inversion of the period, at 1.04 percentage points in July 2023, was followed by two of the better years in the record. Note too that both the merging of brief interruptions and the thirty-day minimum are applied looking backwards: on the first inverted day an investor cannot know which sort of episode he is in.

What the period offers, and what it withholds. Twenty-six years supplies a great deal of data and remarkably few independent events. There are two equity catastrophes, one pandemic, one inflation, three inversions and perhaps six distinct rate cycles. Every claim in the chapters that follow — about regimes, about positioning, about which conditions favour which holdings — rests on a denominator of that size, whatever the number of daily observations may suggest.

This is the reason the arithmetic came first. An investor who knows that a hundred dollars became somewhere between five hundred and eleven hundred almost regardless of where he put it, and that the difference between the best sector and the worst was smaller than the difference between counting dividends and ignoring them, is properly armed against the chapters that follow. He will not expect too much from them.

Sources: sector and commodity funds from end-of-day and thirty-minute price records, split-adjusted throughout, with total return additionally reinvesting dividends; index levels and cyclically adjusted earnings from long-run market records; output, prices and employment from official statistics; the yield curve from fitted zero-coupon Treasury rates. Endpoints differ by series: sector and commodity prices run to 19 August 2026, index levels to 14 August, the yield curve to 7 August, output to the second quarter, prices and employment to July, and the cyclically adjusted ratio only to September 2024. Sector funds are capitalisation-weighted baskets and carry the survivorship of their own index rules; a sector's constituents in 2026 are not those of 2000.

Chapter Two

The Dollar and the Hedge

Diversification is a promise made in calm weather and collected in bad. The only honest test is what it paid on the days one needed it.

I

The dollar is the axis the others turn upon

Of the four currency funds in this book, three are very nearly the same instrument wearing different names. The dollar fund and the euro fund correlate at −0.92, which is less a relationship between two assets than an accounting identity, the euro being the dominant weight in the dollar index. Sterling follows at −0.68. Only the yen stands apart, at −0.42 — and that separation is the entire reason the yen is worth owning, since it is the only one of the four that is not simply the dollar turned upside down.

Every risk asset in the book moves against the dollar, and the ones that move hardest are the ones priced in it. Gold and the gold miners lead at about −0.43, with silver and platinum close behind. Nothing here is even weakly positive. The American sectors cluster far more loosely, between −0.14 and −0.27, and the long Treasury at −0.07 is essentially indifferent to the currency — a useful property in something one holds as a hedge, because it means the hedge is not quietly a second bet on the same thing.

The practical consequence is that an American investor's exposure to the dollar is rarely where he thinks it is. He may hold no currency position at all and still have a substantial one, distributed across his gold, his miners and any foreign equity he owns. The dollar is not a line in his portfolio; it is a factor running through most of the lines he already has, and it is invisible precisely because it never appears under its own name.

II

Only two things reliably rose when equities fell

The proper test of a hedge is not its average correlation but its behaviour on the days one actually needs it, and those are not the same question. An asset can be uncorrelated on average and perfectly useless in a crisis, because averages are dominated by quiet days and crises are not. Sorting nineteen years of sessions into five buckets by the average return of the nine sectors, and reading off what each supposed diversifier did in each bucket, gives an unusually clean answer.

On the worst fifth of days the long Treasury gained forty-one basis points and the yen twenty-five, and both rise monotonically as equities fall. The dollar adds seventeen. That is what a hedge looks like when it is real: not merely positive when it matters, but increasingly positive the more it matters.

Gold does the opposite, and the fact deserves dwelling on because it contradicts the thing gold is chiefly sold for. On the worst fifth of days gold lost eleven basis points; on the best fifth it gained fifteen. Its slope runs the wrong way. At daily frequency gold is not an equity hedge at all — it is a position on the dollar and on real interest rates, which is a perfectly respectable thing to own and an entirely different thing. The investor who holds gold expecting it to catch him on a bad Tuesday has misunderstood what he is holding, and will discover this on precisely the Tuesday he was relying on it.

And then there is high yield, which is not a hedge but its photographic negative. On the worst fifth of days it lost fifty-two basis points — more than the long Treasury gained — and on the best fifth it gained fifty-three. Its slope is steeper than that of most equity sectors. Investment-grade credit, by contrast, runs almost flat across the five buckets, from −6 to +11. The two sit side by side in every asset-allocation table under the heading “bonds”, and one of them is a bond while the other is equity risk wearing a coupon.

III

In 2022 every hedge failed at once

The comfort of a daily average dissolves in the episodes that actually destroy capital. Across seven equity drawdowns the long Treasury rose in five, the yen in five, the dollar in five and gold in four. Investment-grade credit rose in one. High yield rose in none — which is the same statement the quintile chart made, arrived at by a different route and over holding periods measured in months rather than days.

Then there is 2022, when everything failed together. Through the inflation drawdown technology fell thirty-three per cent and the long Treasury fell twenty-nine alongside it. Investment-grade credit fell twenty-one, the yen twenty-two, gold seven. Only the dollar and the commodity index rose. Read that row of the table across and it is difficult to locate the portfolio that was protected.

The negative stock-bond correlation that underwrites nearly every balanced portfolio in existence is not a law of nature. It is a property of a regime in which the shock arrives through growth: demand collapses, equities fall, the central bank cuts, and the bond does its work. When the shock arrives through inflation the central bank does the opposite, and the correlation changes sign. The diversification does not merely weaken. It inverts, and it inverts precisely when it was to be paid for.

This is the most important sentence in the chapter, so it is worth stating without hedging: duration hedges equities against a growth shock and fails against an inflation shock, and this record supports both halves with equal force. An investor whose experience began after 1990 has seen almost nothing but the first kind of shock, and has therefore been drawing a conclusion from a sample containing one regime and calling it a property of the assets.

Regime-dependent
Duration hedges equities against a growth shock and fails against an inflation shock. Both statements are supported here; neither is safe alone.

What Chapter Two establishes

That the dollar is the axis the rest of the book turns upon, and that three of the four currency funds are the same bet made three ways. That duration, the yen and the dollar are the only assets here that reliably rise as equities fall, and that they do so more the worse the day gets. That gold's slope runs the wrong way, and high yield's runs steeply the wrong way — it is an equity substitute that pays a coupon and behaves like one exactly when the coupon stops consoling anybody. And that every one of these relationships is conditional on the kind of shock: in 2022 the whole table failed at once, and any portfolio built on the stock-bond correlation of the preceding thirty years was built on a regime rather than a law.

Chapter Three

The Auction and the Ledger

Every bond that exists is owned by somebody. The interesting question is always who, and on what terms they agreed to hold it.

I

Fifteen times the debt, at an unchanged bid-to-cover

In 2000 the Treasury raised $327 billion through coupon auctions. In 2025 it raised $4.82 trillion. That is roughly a fifteenfold increase in the quantity of paper the market was asked to absorb each year. One might reasonably expect the price of that absorption to have risen — that bidders would demand more, cover the offering more thinly, or on some occasion simply fail to appear. The bid-to-cover ratio in 2000 was 2.45. In 2026 it is 2.59. Across the entire quarter-century it has scarcely left the range 2.4 to 3.0.

This is the most remarkable fact in the chapter and it is almost never reported, because nothing happened. The commentary each month concerns whether a particular auction came a basis point through or above the screen — a quantity that is real, measurable, and of no consequence whatever by the following week. The structural fact, that a fifteenfold increase in supply was absorbed at an unchanged cover ratio for twenty-five years, produces no headline on any single day and is therefore invisible, which is a fair description of most of what actually matters in a market.

II

Who actually took the paper

The cover ratio held constant, but the identity of the buyer changed beyond recognition. In 2008 the primary dealers took 61.5 per cent of every coupon auction; by 2026 they take 12.5. Indirect bidders — foreign central banks, and funds bidding through the dealers — went from 26.6 per cent to about 59. Direct bidders, negligible at 1.7 per cent in 2008, now take 18.3. The dealers, whose entire economic function is to warehouse inventory between issuance and final ownership, have very nearly stopped doing it.

The proximate cause is not mysterious. Post-crisis capital rules made a Treasury on a dealer's balance sheet expensive to hold in a way it had never been before; the supplementary leverage ratio does not distinguish between a Treasury bill and a junk bond for the purposes of its denominator. A dealer asked to hold more government paper against the same equity must either raise the equity or decline the paper. The record here suggests plainly enough which they chose, and it would be strange to blame them: they were responding exactly as the rule intended, and the rule's authors were solving a different problem.

III

The hedge funds became the warehouse

If the dealers stopped holding the inventory, somebody else began to, because the bonds did not cease to exist. The Commitments of Traders report divides the futures market into dealers, asset managers and leveraged money, and its Treasury section tells a story that dovetails precisely with the auction record. In the period to 2015 the three groups were roughly balanced. As of August 2026, expressed in ten-year equivalents, asset managers are long 6.16 million contracts and leveraged money is short 4.81 million.

That is the Treasury basis trade, and it is close to a trillion dollars of gross notional on each side. The asset manager buys the future because it is the cheapest way to own duration. The hedge fund sells him the future, buys the cash bond against it, finances the bond in repo, and earns the small spread between the two. The trade is nearly riskless in the sense that matters to its owner and dangerous in the sense that matters to everyone else, because the entire structure depends on repo remaining available at a price. It is, functionally, the dealer inventory of 2007 relocated to a balance sheet with no capital requirement and no access to the discount window.

Whether that is a problem is not a question this data can answer, and it is worth being clear about the limit. What the ledger shows is that a function which used to be performed by regulated institutions with a central-bank backstop is now performed by unregulated ones without. Whether it breaks is a question about repo markets under stress, which is not measured here and on which the record is silent.

A trap in the source data

The CFTC renamed nearly every contract in its financial-futures report in February 2022. “10-YEAR U.S. TREASURY NOTES” became “UST 10Y NOTE”; the dollar index and the bond contract changed likewise. A series joined on the market name therefore ends on 1 February 2022 and begins again the following week as a new and apparently unrelated instrument.

The contract market code — 043602 for the ten-year, 020601 for the bond — is stable across the rename. Every series here is keyed on the code. Anyone joining these tables by name will lose the last four years without being told.

IV

Two stories, one trend, and no mechanism

The temptation at this point is overwhelming, and it must be resisted. The dealers' share of auctions fell from 61 per cent to 12. The hedge funds' short position grew from nothing to five million contracts. Plotted against one another quarterly since 2008, the two series correlate at 0.763. In a great deal of published research that number would be the end of the investigation and the beginning of the causal claim.

Both series trend for eighteen years, and any two series that trend for eighteen years will correlate. The correct test is whether they move together once the trend is removed. Differenced quarter on quarter, the correlation is −0.055. Over four-quarter changes it is −0.111. There is no quarterly co-movement whatever.

What survives is a description of an era, not a mechanism. Both things demonstrably happened, over the same two decades, for reasons that plausibly connect — and one cannot date the connection, cannot trade it, and cannot say which moved first. That is a real limitation and not a rhetorical concession. A story that explains eighteen years and no individual quarter is a story about history; treated as a model it would fail on its first out-of-sample use.

Trend only
Levels correlate at 0.763; quarterly changes at −0.055. The relationship is real as history and absent as dynamics.
V

What the positioning data will not do is forecast

Having established that the hedge funds hold an enormous short, one naturally asks whether fading it pays. The quartile table is encouraging at first sight. When leveraged money is at its most short, the ten-year Treasury fund returns +0.74 per cent over the following thirteen weeks; when least short, −0.30. Over twenty-six weeks the spread widens. The pattern is monotone across all four buckets, which is precisely the shape that persuades people.

It does not survive its own significance test. There are 989 weekly observations, but they overlap thirteen deep, leaving roughly seventy-six independent ones. Against a circular-shift null — rotating the positioning series against the returns, which preserves its persistence and destroys only its alignment — the observed correlation of −0.119 sits inside a null distribution whose standard deviation is 0.118. The probability of seeing something at least this large by chance is 0.32. For the long fund at twenty-six weeks it is 0.78.

Circular-shift null, 2,000 rotations, seed fixed. A p-value near one half means the observed statistic is indistinguishable from an arbitrary misalignment of the same two series.
FundHorizonObserved corr.Null s.d.pEffective nVerdict

This is a negative result and it is worth as much as a positive one. The positioning data describes the market's structure with great precision. It does not predict the market's direction — and the monotone quartile table, the very thing that makes it appear to, is exactly what one should expect to find in a persistent series tested against overlapping windows, whether or not any relationship exists at all. That last clause is the whole trouble with this kind of evidence: the pattern is produced by the method, not by the market, and it looks identical either way.

The auction reprices the market; it does not forecast it

Auction days are commonly said to weigh on the long end, and the raw average appears to agree. On the 723 sessions carrying a ten-, twenty- or thirty-year auction, the long Treasury fund returned −3.9 basis points against roughly +1.0 on other sessions. The gap of nearly five basis points is the familiar concession — the market cheapening the paper to make room for it. It is also, with a daily standard deviation of ninety-six basis points, a t-statistic of −1.23. One would obtain a gap this large by chance about one time in five. The concession may well be real; nineteen years of daily data are simply not enough to demonstrate it at this effect size.

What is unambiguous is the reaction to the result once it is known. Sorting auctions by how their cover ratio compared with the trailing twenty of the same tenor, the long fund returned −17.6 basis points when the cover came in weak and +18.4 when it came in strong — a spread of 36.0 basis points with a standard error of 8.8, better than four standard deviations. Even after a ten-basis-point round trip, twenty-six of those thirty-six survive.

And it is entirely out of reach. Results are published at one o'clock; the same-day close already contains them. Measured on the following day — the only window an investor could actually trade — the spread reverses to −9.6 basis points with a standard error of 9.1, a t-statistic of −1.05. Charge the round trip and it becomes −19.6. The information is real, large, and fully impounded before anyone reading about it can act; what remains for him to trade is the privilege of paying ten basis points to take the wrong side of a coin.

Reaction confirmed
Same-day response to cover surprise: 36.0 bp, t = 4.11. Survives the round trip — but nobody can reach it.
Not tradeable
Next-day continuation: −9.6 bp, t = −1.05, and −19.6 bp after cost. Published at one o'clock, priced by four.

What Chapter Three establishes

That the American government sold fifteen times as much debt across twenty-five years without the price of selling it moving at all. That the buyer changed completely while the price did not — dealers from 61 per cent to 12, foreign and indirect bidders from 27 to 59. That the warehousing function the dealers abandoned has been taken up by leveraged funds running a trillion-dollar basis trade financed in repo. That the connection between those two facts is an eighteen-year trend and not a quarterly mechanism. And that none of this magnificent ledger forecasts anything: neither the positioning, nor the auction, nor the concession that everyone believes in and nobody can demonstrate.

Chapter Four

The Circle and the Spot

A picture is a hypothesis wearing better clothes. The only respectful thing to do with a good one is to try to break it.

This chapter began as a metaphor rather than a measurement. Suppose the market is a circle: at rest the sectors sit at some common distance from its centre, moving together with the whole. Occasionally a part of it erupts, the way a spot erupts on the surface of the sun — one sector detaches and does something of its own. And then, over some interval, the circle re-forms.

That is a picture, and pictures are cheap. What follows is an attempt to make it expensive: to define the circle so it can be measured, the eruption so it can be counted, and the re-formation so it can be tested against the possibility that nothing is restoring anything at all. Three of the four propositions survive. The fourth — and it is the one that would have been worth money — comes within a whisker and does not.

I

Defining the circle so it can be wrong

The centre of the market is not a metaphor; it is the first eigenvector of the sector correlation matrix. Taking a rolling quarter of daily total returns for the nine long-lived sectors and decomposing their correlations, the leading component is the thing every sector has in common — the market itself. How much of the total variance it claims is a direct measure of how far the circle has collapsed towards a point.

Each sector's distance from that centre — its radius — is the share of its variance the market factor fails to explain. A sector that is nothing but the market has radius zero and sits at the centre. A sector that goes entirely its own way has radius one and sits far out. If the market were a true circle, all nine radii would be equal.

They are not, and this is the first thing the measurement corrects in the picture. Averaged over twenty-six years the radii run from 0.62 for utilities and 0.54 for energy down to 0.15 for industrials. Utilities and energy live at the rim, going about their own business — one answering to interest rates, the other to the price of oil. Industrials sit almost at the centre, because industrials very nearly are the market. The resting shape is not a circle but a lopsided ellipse, and it is lopsided in a way that makes economic sense.

The circle does collapse, and it collapses hardest exactly where one would hope. The market factor's share of variance averages 0.64. Its lowest reading, 0.23, falls in September 2000, when the technology unwind had the sectors moving in every direction at once and there was scarcely a common market to speak of. Its highest, 0.93, falls in the week of 16 March 2020, when there was nothing but a common market: nine sectors, one trade, no diversification available at any price.

That is the second proposition confirmed, and it is worth stating as a warning rather than a curiosity. Correlation is not a constant an investor can look up; it is a quantity that rises precisely when he needs it to fall. A portfolio built on the average of that number is not diversified in the state that matters, because in that state the number is not near its average.

II

The spots are real, and they are not on a cycle

Counting the eruptions requires a definition that does not flatter the story. A burst here is a sector whose radius rises at least two standard deviations above its own trailing year, and it ends when the radius falls back inside one. On that definition the record contains 200 bursts across nine sectors in twenty-six years, with a median duration of 43 sessions — about two months.

They are not evenly distributed, and the distribution is informative. Financials burst most often, twenty-nine times, and industrials twenty-six — both sectors that normally sit near the centre, so detaching is a larger event for them. Energy bursts least often, seventeen times, but its bursts last longest by a wide margin, a median of eighty-nine sessions. A sector that lives at the rim erupts rarely and stays erupted; one that lives at the centre erupts often and returns quickly.

The sun's spots run on an eleven-year cycle, and it would be pleasing if the market's did too. They do not. Taking the quarterly burst count and looking for periodicity, the strongest cycle in the series has a period of 0.7 years, and its power sits at the p = 0.99 point of a shuffled null — which is to say the series is less periodic than a random permutation of itself. The analogy carries as far as the eruption and no further. Whatever else the market is, it is not keeping time.

III

The circle re-forms, but nothing is pushing it

Here is where the metaphor makes its strongest claim and where it has to be handled most carefully. “It takes effort to bring the circle back” asserts a restoring force — something actively pulling the erupted sector home. And the raw measurement appears to agree: from the peak of a burst the radius z-score falls from 2.85 to half that within 13 sessions, and reaches zero by about sixty-three. The circle demonstrably re-forms, and it does so on a timescale of weeks.

But a statistic selected on a high reading must come down whether or not anything is pulling it. This is regression to the mean, and it is the oldest trap in quantitative work: pick the tallest men in a room and their sons will be shorter, with no force acting on anybody. The question is therefore not whether the radius falls — it must — but whether it falls faster than the same series would with no restoring force at all.

Against the better-specified null, it does not. Circularly shifting each sector's radius series — which preserves its own persistence exactly and destroys only its alignment to the calendar — and re-detecting bursts in the shuffled data produces a median half-life of 12 sessions against the observed 13. The observed restoration is, if anything, marginally slower than chance. The probability of the null producing a half-life at least this fast is 1.00.

A second null disagrees, and the disagreement has to be reported rather than resolved by preference. Fitting an AR(1) process to each radius series and simulating from it gives a median half-life of 19 sessions, against which the observed 13 looks fast, at p < 0.001. But the fitted persistence is 0.99 or higher for every sector — very nearly a unit root — and an AR(1) that persistent is simply a poor description of the real series, which decays faster at short lags than any single-parameter model allows. The circular-shift null uses the actual series and needs no model. Where two nulls disagree and one of them required a fitted assumption, the honest reading is the one that did not.

So the third proposition survives in its weak form and fails in its strong one. The circle re-forms — that is observable, and the timescale is real. But nothing is pushing it. The re-formation is the arithmetic of a persistent noisy quantity returning to its own mean, and a shuffled copy of the same data re-forms just as quickly. “It takes effort” is the part the measurement cannot support.

Re-forms, but unforced
Half-life 13 sessions against a shuffled null's 12. The circle returns; nothing observable is returning it.
IV

The one thing in this book that nearly worked

A burst is an event in variance, which says nothing at all about direction — so the trading question is a separate one, and it produced the only encouraging number in these chapters. Splitting each burst by the sign of the sector's market-adjusted move and taking the opposite side, held for a quarter and charged ten basis points a round trip, returns +1.33 per cent per burst. On the 162 bursts with a complete forward window that is a naive t-statistic of +2.77.

The naive statistic is wrong, and correcting it is the first thing to do rather than the last. Sectors erupt together; 2008 and 2020 supply a great many bursts between them, and at a sixty-three-session horizon the forward windows of nearby bursts overlap almost entirely. The 162 observations are not 162 independent facts. Collapsing them to quarterly means gives sixty-six effective observations, and on those the mean is +1.71 per cent with a clustered t of +2.63, p = 0.011. Smaller, and still there.

It then survives the tests that killed everything else in this book. Shifting the entire burst calendar circularly against the returns, two thousand times, puts the observed result at the 0.022 point one-sided. Removing 2008, 2009 and 2020 from the sample altogether leaves +1.40 per cent at a clustered t of +2.37 — so it is not a crisis artefact. No single quarter supplies more than thirteen per cent of the profit. Split in half by calendar, both halves are positive, the first at t = 3.22 and the second at t = 1.67: decaying, but not reversing, which is a materially different thing from the thirteen-of-sixteen sign flip that ended the macro study.

And it is not short-term reversal wearing a costume, which was the control I most expected it to fail. Fading extreme relative performance is a documented effect requiring no geometry whatever, so the matched naive signal — same dates, same holding period, same cost, but selecting the biggest relative mover instead of the bursting sector — is the test that decides whether the circle is doing any work. It returns +0.60 per cent at a clustered t of +0.50: nothing. Run on every month rather than only burst dates, +0.10 per cent, t = 0.31: nothing. And the two signals barely overlap — on a burst date, the bursting sector is the biggest relative mover only 16 per cent of the time. The radius is selecting something the return alone does not see.

And then it fails, on the same bar that this book has applied to everything else. The sleeve's annualised Sharpe ratio is 0.65. Forty-four distinct configurations have now been evaluated across these chapters, and at the dispersion actually observed across out-of-sample paths — 0.43 — the best of forty-four draws from a population of worthless strategies would be expected to show a Sharpe of 0.96. The burst trade falls short of that by 0.31.

This is the most uncomfortable result in the book and the one most worth sitting with. It is not a failure of the kind the macro overlays produced — those lost money and reversed sign. This one makes money, survives clustering, survives a circular-shift null, survives the removal of every crisis, and is demonstrably not the well-known effect it most resembles. On any conventional standard it would be published. It fails only against the count of things that were tried before it was found, and that count is not a technicality: it is the honest price of having gone looking.

The correct disposition is neither to claim it nor to discard it. A result at p = 0.011 that clears its controls and misses its multiple-testing bar by a third of a Sharpe is exactly the shape of thing that should be written down, pre-registered with its threshold fixed in advance, and tested forward on data that does not yet exist. If it is real it will still be there in three years, measured once, against a bar of one trial rather than forty-four. If it is not, three years will cost nothing but the waiting.

Promising, not established
Clustered p = 0.011, shift-null p = 0.022, survives crisis removal, not explained by reversal — and 0.31 of a Sharpe short of the forty-four-trial hurdle.

What Chapter Four establishes

That the circle exists and can be measured: the market factor claims 64 per cent of sector variance on average, 23 per cent at its most dispersed and 93 per cent in the week of 16 March 2020, when nine sectors briefly became one trade. That the resting shape is an ellipse rather than a circle, with utilities and energy at the rim and industrials nearly at the centre, for reasons that are economic rather than statistical. That the spots are real — two hundred of them, median duration two months — and that they follow no cycle whatever, the burst count being less periodic than a shuffle of itself.

That the circle re-forms in about thirteen sessions, but that nothing observable is forcing it: a shuffled copy of the same data re-forms in twelve. And that fading a burst has made money, survived every control including the one designed to catch it impersonating short-term reversal, and still sits a third of a Sharpe below the bar that forty-four trials demand. That last item is the book's only near miss, and near misses are worth recording precisely because the temptation to round them up is so strong.

Chapter Five

Everything That Did Not Work

A table that confirms what you hoped is not evidence. It is a mirror, and the face in it is your own.

The three preceding chapters produced a number of facts and a smaller number of temptations. This chapter is about the temptations. Each of them arrived in the same costume — a monotone table, an ordering that made sense, a backtest that beat its benchmark — and each of them, put to a test it had not been designed to pass, came apart. They are collected here rather than dismissed in passing because the manner of their failure is more instructive than any of the facts in the earlier chapters, and because a reader who learns only the facts will go on to make these same four mistakes on data of his own.

I

A study that does not survive its own table

The macro-regime study asks whether the state of policy, credit and volatility can tell an investor when to stand aside. It is careful work. Signals are lagged two trading days so that nothing is used before it could have been known; the discovery period runs to 2016 and the validation period from 2017; the report states openly that its thresholds have since been inspected and that its validation rows are no longer pristine holdouts. On method it is more scrupulous than most of what is published on the subject.

Its own validation table nonetheless reports that not one of the sixteen signals beat buying and holding. Not one. The median signal gave up 4.58 percentage points of annual return. Three of the sixteen improved the Sharpe ratio — by 0.01, 0.01 and 0.06, margins that would vanish under any resampling. And the median improvement in maximum drawdown, which is the entire justification for accepting a lower return in the first place, was zero. The overlays gave up return and did not buy protection with it.

The tables were there all along. What was missing was a sentence saying what they added up to. This is the commonest failure in quantitative research and it is not dishonesty; it is the natural result of presenting sixteen rows neutrally and letting the reader form an impression. The impression a reader forms from sixteen neutrally-presented rows is that some of them worked.

The deeper failure is in the forward-return table, and it is a failure of sign rather than of size. For each signal one may ask whether the “risk-off” state actually preceded weaker three-month returns than the normal state. In the discovery period eight of sixteen did. In the validation period, thirteen of the sixteen reversed direction. Signals that marked danger before 2017 marked opportunity after it, and the reverse.

A thirteen-of-sixteen reversal is worse news than a random result would be. If these signals carried no information one would expect about eight to flip. Thirteen suggests the discovery-period patterns were not merely noise but noise fitted closely enough to invert. And the mean edge is negative in both periods — −0.26 points in discovery and −0.69 in validation — meaning that on average, across both halves of a twenty-six-year sample, the states labelled risk-off were followed by higher returns than the states labelled normal. The signal is not weak. It points the other way, and always did.

Does not replicate
0 of 16 signals beat buy-and-hold on return; median drawdown improvement 0.0pp; 13 of 16 reversed sign between discovery and validation.

What that chapter should have concluded

The finding is not that macro conditions do not matter. It is that the tradeable content of a publicly known macro state is approximately nil. An inverted curve genuinely does precede recessions. It does not follow that an investor can profitably sell equities when the curve inverts, because the curve stays inverted for years at a stretch, every participant can see that it is inverted, and the interval between the signal and the event is longer than most investors' patience and all of their performance-measurement periods. A fact can be true, well-documented, and completely useless to the person holding it, and the distinction between those three properties is the whole of this book.

The study's own best instinct — that six tightening cycles are six observations wearing six thousand costumes — deserved to be its conclusion rather than a caveat in its closing section. Sixteen signals crossed with two periods and three indices is on the order of a hundred cells, and the companion study on the same data applies a maximum-gap circular-shift correction for exactly that reason. The regime study does not apply it to itself. That single change would improve it more than any other, and on the evidence of its own tables the result of making the change is not seriously in doubt.

II

What ten basis points destroys, and what it does not

Every result so far has been quoted gross, and gross returns are a courtesy the market does not extend. A round trip in a liquid American exchange-traded fund — the spread crossed twice, plus commission — costs on the order of ten basis points. The convention used here is five basis points on each one-way notional traded, so that a complete buy-and-sell cycle costs the stated ten.

The answer depends almost entirely on turnover, and turnover varies across these strategies by a factor of three hundred. Buying thirty-one funds in 2007 and holding them to 2026 spreads the whole ten basis points over nineteen years, which is half a basis point a year and beneath the resolution of any table in this book. Rebalancing nine sectors to equal weight every day runs 146 per cent turnover and costs seven basis points against an effect of eighty-one. Standing aside whenever the VIX exceeds twenty runs 1,727 per cent and costs eighty-six — but it also gives up nine and a half points of return, so the cost is comfortably the least of its problems.

Which yields the most useful single sentence in this chapter: cost was almost never the binding constraint. The median macro overlay lost thirteen hundredths of a point a year to friction and four and a half points to being wrong. An analyst who reaches for trading costs as his explanation is very often avoiding a harder one, and the harder one is usually that the idea did not work.

Independent reconstruction of the regime study's overlays on the S&P 500 total-return index, validation period 2017 onward. Buy-and-hold returns 15.74 per cent. Switch counts and exposures reproduce the original to within a switch or two, which is the check that the replication is faithful.
SignalSwitchesCAGR @2bpCAGR @10bpCost dragvs buy & hold
Cost is not the objection
Median drag from raising the charge fivefold: 0.13pp a year. Median shortfall against buy-and-hold: 4.65pp. These signals were never close.
III

The strategy that beat the wrong benchmark, and then the wrong basis

Sector momentum looked, on first inspection, like the one exception in the book. Holding the three sectors with the strongest twelve-month return, rebalanced monthly, appeared to return 7.54 per cent a year net of cost against 6.68 for the cap-weighted index — an apparent eighty-six basis points for a strategy whose turnover costs only twenty-seven. It was the only thing tested anywhere in these chapters that beat the index after charges, and for about a day it looked like the finding.

It was measured on the wrong basis. Those figures came from price-only series. A rotation that chases twelve-month winners spends a good deal of its time in higher-yielding sectors, so discarding dividends penalised the strategy less than it penalised the benchmark — no, worse than that: it penalised them differently, and nobody could say in advance in which direction. Recomputed on total return, momentum earns 9.43 per cent a year net against 9.48 for the S&P 500 total-return index. It does not beat the index by eighty-six basis points. It loses to it by five.

And it was also measured against the wrong benchmark. A rotation among nine sectors is not a cap-weighted portfolio and cannot honestly be judged against one; the correct comparison is the equal-weight portfolio of the same nine assets, which is precisely what the strategy collapses to if its signal carries no information. Measured that way the excess is +0.19 per cent a year, an information ratio of 0.02. Across seven configurations the excess ranges from -0.98 to +1.11 per cent with no pattern in lookback or breadth, and the best of them returns a one-sided p of 0.17 against a circular-shift null. The expected information ratio from taking the best of seven zero-edge configurations is 0.16 — higher than the 0.12 actually observed.

Two independent measurement choices, neither made in bad faith, between them manufactured the entire finding. This is the most instructive failure in the book, because nothing about the strategy looked wrong: the returns were real, the costs were paid, the sample ran twenty-five years, the turnover was modest for what it was doing. The error was upstream of the analysis in both cases — in what the series measured, and in what it was placed beside.

The general form is worth naming, because it is everywhere. Whenever a strategy differs from its benchmark in more than one way at once — here a timing signal, a weighting scheme, and a dividend treatment — the comparison quietly attributes the whole difference to whichever of them the analyst happens to be interested in. The remedy is not statistical sophistication. It is the duller discipline of asking what the strategy would return with its signal replaced by noise, and measuring against that.

Monthly rebalancing, net of a 10bp round trip, measured against an equal-weight portfolio of the same nine sectors. Positive and negative configurations are interleaved across lookback and breadth, which is the signature of noise rather than of a decaying but real effect.
ConfigurationExcess over equal weightInformation ratio
IV

The machine cannot find it either

A reasonable objection to everything so far is that the signals were chosen by hand, one at a time, by someone who may simply have chosen badly. The remedy is to hand every series in these chapters to a method with no preconceptions and far more patience: a gradient-boosted tree given thirty-four features — the policy rate and its changes, both curve spreads, the volatility level and its term structure, the credit spread, the three positioning z-scores, the auction cover and absorption measures, and momentum and realised volatility for seven assets — and asked to predict the next month's return.

The validation has to be built to stop the model cheating, because a naive split guarantees that it will. A twenty-one-day forward return observed on Monday overlaps the one observed on Tuesday, so a randomly chosen test row is very nearly present in the training set already. The folds here are therefore contiguous in time; any training row whose forward window overlaps a test window is purged; and a further twenty-one-day embargo is dropped after each test block. What remains is 4,560 sessions from 2008 onward and one honest out-of-sample prediction for each.

The out-of-sample coefficient of determination is negative for all three targets. A negative R² is not a weak result. It is a statement that the model would have done better to predict the historical mean and go home. On the S&P 500 the rank correlation between prediction and outcome is +0.005; against a null built by circularly shifting the entire feature matrix against the labels and repeating the whole purged cross-validation two hundred times, the probability of a correlation at least that large in absolute value is 0.95. For the ten-year Treasury fund it is 0.82, and for the long fund 0.17.

Running every combination of test blocks rather than a single partition shows exactly why one backtest is worth so little. Taking all twenty-eight ways of choosing two of eight time-blocks as the test set produces twenty-eight out-of-sample paths. On the S&P the mean net Sharpe across them is +0.14 with a standard deviation of 0.51, and the fifth to ninety-fifth percentile runs from −0.94 to +0.66. On the ten-year fund exactly half the paths are positive. An analyst who ran one backtest and published it would, depending on nothing but which slice he happened to draw, have reported a convincing success or a convincing failure from identical data and an identical model. He would not have been careless. He would have done what is normally done.

The feature-importance table carries the clearest confession of all. Permuting a single feature and repeating the entire purged cross-validation measures how much that feature was genuinely carrying. Twenty of the thirty-four features have positive importance and fourteen negative — that is, for fourteen of them, destroying the information improved the out-of-sample prediction. And the largest single importance, three months of S&P momentum at 0.023, is several times the model's entire out-of-sample correlation of 0.005. A model whose most valuable input matters more than the model's total accuracy is not decomposing a signal into parts. It is redistributing noise.

One caution on reading that chart: the scatter behind each bar is across the five permutation repeats, which measures how reproducibly the shuffling bites. It is not a standard error on the data and must not be read as one — a feature can have a perfectly stable importance and still be worthless.

V

Paying for the search

There remains one bill outstanding, and it is the one most often left unpaid: the search itself. Forty-four distinct configurations were evaluated across these chapters — three machine-learning targets, seven momentum specifications, eleven macro overlays at two cost levels each, three auction trades, and nine burst-fade variants. Every one of them was a draw from a distribution. The best of forty-four draws from a population of genuinely worthless strategies is not zero; it is a positive number, and a large one, and reporting it as though it were a single hypothesis is the most widespread error in quantitative finance.

With a dispersion of 0.43 across the combinatorial paths, the expected maximum from forty-four zero-skill trials is a Sharpe of 0.96. The best result anywhere in this book is the burst trade of Chapter Four at 0.65, and it misses by 0.31. Nothing tested here clears the bar that pure luck would clear.

That is a stronger claim than saying the strategies were bad, and it should be read carefully. It does not say they have no edge. It says the evidence assembled — twenty-five years, thirty-one instruments, seven thousand auctions, a thousand positioning reports — is insufficient to distinguish them from strategies with no content whatever. Twenty-six years is a long time to a person and a small sample to a statistician, and that discrepancy is the source of more confident nonsense than any other fact in finance.

Nothing clears the hurdle
Negative out-of-sample R² on all three targets, null p-values of 0.95, 0.82 and 0.17, and a best Sharpe of 0.65 against a luck threshold of 0.96.
VI

What the ledger shows

Six findings across these four chapters survived testing and six did not, and the line between them is the only conclusion worth carrying away. What held: the dividend is worth about two points a year and appears on no price chart. A bond fund's return is its coupon. The dollar is the axis every other asset turns upon. Duration and the yen rise when equities fall, and gold does not. All of those hedges failed together in 2022. And the Treasury sold fifteen times as much paper across a quarter of a century without the price of selling it moving at all, while the identity of the buyer changed beyond recognition.

What did not hold is, without a single exception, everything that promised to be useful. Positioning does not forecast. The auction concession cannot be demonstrated, and the part of the auction that can be measured is priced before anyone can reach it. The dealer retreat and the hedge-fund warehouse share an eighteen-year trend and no mechanism. The macro overlays lose four and a half points a year and reverse sign out of sample. Sector momentum's edge was equal weighting wearing a hat. And a machine with thirty-four features and 4,560 purged sessions produced a negative out-of-sample R² on every target it was given.

Two methodological lessons deserve to outlive the particular numbers. The first is that trading cost was almost never the binding constraint — thirteen hundredths of a point to friction against four and a half to being wrong — so cost is the last thing to check and not the first. The second is that the benchmark decides the answer more often than the strategy does. Momentum beat the cap-weighted index and lost to the equal-weight portfolio of the very same nine assets, and only one of those two comparisons was honest.

Three errors were found in this book's own first draft, and every one of them flattered the result. An equal-weight series and the index it was measured against were based one trading day apart, on either side of a 3.8 per cent fall. A column of overlay returns was silently shifted by one against its headings. A same-day and a next-day auction statistic were computed on samples of different size and then compared with each other. None of these was a subtle statistical matter. Each was a clerical slip of the kind that survives review because the number it produces looks reasonable, and each has been left visible in the text rather than quietly repaired, because a reader who never sees the corrections will assume there were none to make.

The distinction an investor must learn to draw is between a fact about the market's structure and a fact about its direction. These chapters are unusually rich in the first and, after every test that could be applied to them, entirely empty of the second. That ratio is not an artefact of these particular datasets. The structure is observable, stable and free. The direction is none of those things — and the tables that appear to offer it, the monotone quartile, the striking cell, the backtest that beat the index, are on nearly every occasion one takes the trouble to test them offering something else entirely.

Chapter Six · Institutional conviction

The Research Department You Do Not Pay For

Fourteen concentrated managers, 617 quarterly filings, 26,794 disclosed holdings. Their books beat the index. Their new positions, bought when a follower could actually buy them, do not. And yet the one thing a trend-following system needs is there anyway — in the shape of the distribution rather than its centre.

2013-06 → 2026-0614 managers617 filings 1,169 companies94.4% of value matched

A concentrated manager putting eight per cent of his fund into one company has done something a systematic screen never does: he has formed an opinion about that specific business. Months of work usually sit behind it — the unit economics, the competitors, the balance sheet, calls with customers and former employees. The position size is itself a statement, and it is a statement about one company rather than about the environment. That is what makes it different in kind from a volatility regime or a dealer-gamma reading, and it is why the idea deserved a proper test rather than an argument.

The test asks three questions in order, because the later ones are worthless if the earlier ones fail. Do these managers' disclosed books actually outperform? Can a follower capture any of it, given that the filing arrives forty-five days late? And — the question that matters for a breakout system — do breakouts in recently accumulated names have a fatter right tail than ordinary breakouts?

The answers are yes, no, and yes. The middle one is the reason the third is interesting rather than redundant.

I

Track the filer, not the famous name

The first thing that broke was the manager list itself, and it broke in exactly the way one would predict and still not catch. A 13F is filed by a legal entity, not by a person, and the entity is frequently not named after anyone the press writes about. Resolving thirty-four managers by searching EDGAR for the name each is known by produced seven wrong answers, two of them silent.

Berkshire Hathaway resolved to “Feltz Wealth PLAN Inc.” — a real filer, forty-one filings, entirely the wrong one. Royce resolved to Legg Mason, its former parent. Wasatch resolved to “Curated Wealth Partners”. Baupost matched a five-filing registration rather than the hundred-and-five-filing entity, and Fundsmith a single-filing fund rather than the LLP.

Two returned nothing at all, and those are the instructive ones. Baron Capital files as BAMCO Inc. Mohnish Pabrai files as Dalal Street, LLC. Neither contains the name anyone would search for. Had the study proceeded on the first search result, it would have measured Legg Mason's book and called it Royce's, and omitted Baron and Pabrai entirely, and every downstream number would have been confidently wrong.

The seven corrections. Each was found by checking the filing count and the entity name against what the manager is known to be, rather than by trusting the first search hit — which is the only check available when there is no authoritative name-to-CIK map for investment advisers.
ManagerWhat the name search returnedThe actual filerCIK
What the join costs

A 13F identifies a holding by CUSIP; the price history is keyed on ticker; there is no free map between them. The join therefore runs on issuer name, which is lossy in three specific ways a first attempt got wrong: the names arrive HTML-escaped, so S&amp;P GLOBAL never matched S&P Global; filers abbreviate heavily (NATL, RY, SYS, LABS, RESH); and stripping legal suffixes indiscriminately also strips real words.

Corrected, the join captures 94.4 per cent of reported dollar value across 21,061 of 26,794 holding rows. What remains unmatched is dominated by private holdings — SpaceX is the largest single line — and punctuation cases. The match rate is reported per manager rather than in aggregate, because a manager whose names matched poorly would otherwise be judged on an unrepresentative slice of its own book.

II

The books do beat the index. The filings do not.

Value-weighting the disclosed holdings of all fourteen managers and measuring forward against the S&P 500 total-return index, the books win. Over four quarters the disclosed positions beat the index by 5.43 per cent measured from the quarter the filing describes, and by 3.93 per cent measured from the date the filing became public. These are real managers with real records and the aggregate reflects it.

That number should nevertheless be treated with suspicion, because the universe it is measured in contains only securities that still have a listing today. A position that went to zero and was delisted is simply absent from the price file, and therefore absent from the average. Survivorship flatters the manager side of every comparison in this chapter. It is worth saying at the outset which findings that bias could be manufacturing and which it could not: it inflates any positive result, and it makes any negative result conservative.

Now the question that matters, which is not whether they are good but whether their disclosures are usable. Isolating positions that are genuinely new — present in this filing and absent from the last — and measuring from the month after the filing became public, the answer is a clean no. New positions underperform the index by 3.39 per cent over the following four quarters, with a t-statistic of −2.31, and by 5.67 per cent over eight.

The forty-five-day lag is not a friction on the edge. It is larger than the edge. Measured from the quarter-end the filing describes — which no outsider can trade — new positions are slightly positive at one quarter, +1.58 per cent with a t of 2.17. Measured from the date anyone could actually have acted, the same positions are negative at every horizon. The gap runs between 2.4 and 4.1 percentage points, and it is the whole of the effect.

III

A finding that reversed when the sort was fixed

The proposed screen was $300m to $5bn — the band where an institutional footprint should matter most and coverage is thinnest. Sorted by market capitalisation as it stands today, the data appeared to demolish that idea, and demolish it beautifully: companies now under $300m returned −22.6 per cent against the index with a t of −4.14, rising monotonically to +6.9 per cent for companies now over $50bn. Smaller is worse, cleanly, across five buckets.

That result is an artefact, and the artefact is instructive enough to keep in the chapter rather than quietly delete. Market capitalisation today is a post-outcome variable. A company whose shares fell eighty per cent after the manager bought it is small now because the position went badly. Sorting on it guarantees the small buckets fill with losers, and the monotone ladder is manufactured by the sort rather than found in the data.

Reconstructing market capitalisation as at the filing — scaling today's figure back on the adjusted price — the ladder disappears entirely. Companies under $300m at the time of purchase returned +17.3 per cent; the worst band is $2–5bn at −6.3; the largest companies are also negative at −4.4. There is no monotone size effect. There is no reliable size effect at all.

The reconstruction is itself imperfect and the imperfection should be named. Scaling by price assumes a constant share count, which is wrong for precisely the small companies most likely to issue equity. But it is wrong in a way unrelated to the outcome, which the previous version was not, and that distinction is the entire difference between a usable control and a manufactured result.

IV

One manager is sixty per cent of the sample

Baron's filing entity discloses hundreds of positions a quarter, so it supplies 757 of the 1,252 new positions in the study. Any aggregate is therefore mostly a statement about Baron, and Baron's new positions are the worst in the group at −7.2 per cent with a t of −3.95. That is not a criticism of the firm — a manager holding four hundred names is running a different business from one holding eleven, and its 13F is a portfolio report rather than a set of convictions.

Removing it changes the headline from significantly negative to indistinguishable from zero. The remaining thirteen managers' new positions return +2.44 per cent over four quarters with a t of 1.01. Eight of the fourteen managers have a positive mean. Neither number is evidence of an edge; both are evidence that the aggregate was being driven by breadth rather than by skill, and that pooling a 400-name book with an 11-name book was the wrong operation in the first place.

Conviction, which is the one thing that survives

Position size is a statement, and it is the only statement in the filing that carries information here. Excluding Baron and sorting the remaining new positions by their weight in the manager's book, positions above ten per cent returned +14.3 per cent against the index over four quarters, with a t of +2.33. Positions under two per cent returned −1.3. The ladder is not perfectly monotone — the 5–10 per cent bucket is flat — but the ends are far apart and the direction is the one the hypothesis predicted.

This is worth more than it first appears, because it is a cheap test that separates signal from disclosure. A 13F reports everything above a threshold, and most of what it reports is not a conviction. The weight column tells you which lines the manager actually cared about, and only those lines carry any forward information at all.

V

The right tail, which is the whole question

Everything so far has been about averages, and averages are the wrong statistic for a trend-following system. A breakout book does not hold a position to its mean outcome. It stops out of the left tail at a fixed multiple of volatility and rides the right tail as far as it goes. A distribution with an unchanged centre and fatter tails is worth nothing to a buy-and-hold investor and a great deal to a trend follower, because he keeps one tail and cuts the other.

So the question is not whether accumulated names return more on average. It is whether their breakouts have a larger right tail than ordinary breakouts. A breakout here is a monthly close at a twelve-month high — the honest monthly analogue of a fifty-five-day channel, and the closest this data can reach. The two populations are drawn from the same universe in the same months under the same definition; the only difference is whether a concentrated manager had opened a position in the name within the previous four quarters, dated from when the filing became public.

The answer is yes, and it is not marginal. Of ordinary breakouts, 10.4 per cent returned more than fifty per cent over the following year. Of breakouts in recently accumulated names, 15.2 per cent did — and of breakouts in names accumulated at five per cent or more of the manager's book, 17.4 per cent. The share returning more than twenty-five per cent runs 23.3, 33.0 and 33.6 per cent across the same three groups. Median forward return rises from +4.6 to +9.5 to +11.1.

And the mean says none of this, which is exactly the point. Measured as excess over the average breakout that month, the difference in means carries a t-statistic of −0.57 — nothing, and negative at that, because a handful of catastrophic left-tail outcomes dominate the average. An analyst looking only at mean excess return would report no effect and move on. The effect is entirely in the shape.

Breakouts cluster in time, so the comparison has to control for the month. A good year for breakouts is a good year for all of them, and if accumulated names happened to break out in better months the difference would be calendar rather than skill. The null therefore holds the months and the counts fixed and redraws the tickers at random: same months, same number of breakouts in each, chosen from everything that broke out that month.

Against that null the tail difference survives. The observed gap in the share exceeding fifty per cent is +1.29 percentage points on the month-adjusted measure, against a null mean of +0.37 and a standard deviation of 0.42. The one-sided probability is 0.015. This is the first thing in the book to clear a properly constructed null with room to spare.

Right tail confirmed
15.2% of accumulated breakouts exceed +50% against 10.4% of ordinary ones; 17.4% at high conviction. Month-matched null p = 0.015.
The mean is worthless
Difference in mean excess return: t = −0.57. Anyone measuring the average would conclude there is nothing here.
VI

What this licenses, and what it does not

The architecture the evidence supports is the one that separates selection from timing, and it is not the one that copies the trade. Following a concentrated manager into a new position on the filing date loses money — that is measured, not asserted. What the filing supplies is not an entry. It is a watchlist: a set of companies that somebody with a research budget and a concentrated book has formed an opinion about. The price then decides whether and when to act, and the volatility unit decides how much.

Three limits have to travel with the result, and the first is the most serious. The breakout here is a twelve-month high on monthly closes because that is the only price history available across five thousand small and mid caps. A fifty-five-day channel on daily bars is a different instrument, and whether the tail effect survives at that frequency is untested. Second, the universe contains only securities still listed today, which flatters both populations — though it flatters them equally, which is why the comparison between them is the defensible part and the absolute levels are not. Third, market capitalisation at the filing is reconstructed rather than observed.

And the search has to be paid for here as everywhere else in this book. This chapter evaluated roughly a dozen configurations on top of the forty-four already spent. The tail result clears its own null at p = 0.015, which is genuine, but it is one finding out of many attempts and it has not been tested forward on data that did not exist when it was found. The correct disposition is the same as for the burst trade: write the threshold down now — a new position, at or above five per cent of a concentrated manager's disclosed book, breaking to a channel high within four quarters of the filing date — and let the next three years judge it against a bar of one trial rather than fifty.

What Chapter Six establishes

That the filing entity is not the famous name: seven of thirty-four managers resolved wrongly by name, and Baron and Pabrai not at all, because they file as BAMCO and Dalal Street. That the disclosed books of fourteen concentrated managers beat the index over the period, and that this is partly survivorship. That their new positions, entered when a follower could actually enter them, do not — and that the forty-five-day lag costs between 2.4 and 4.1 percentage points, which is more than the whole effect.

That a clean monotone size finding evaporated when the sort was moved from today's market capitalisation to the market capitalisation at the time of purchase. That conviction survives where size does not: new positions above ten per cent of a book return +14.3 per cent against the index, t = 2.33.

And that the question the strategy actually asks — whether breakouts in recently accumulated names have a fatter right tail — is answered yes. 15.2 per cent of them exceed +50 per cent against 10.4 per cent of ordinary breakouts, 17.4 per cent at high conviction, at a month-matched p of 0.015, while the difference in means is indistinguishable from zero. The edge is in the shape of the distribution, and a system that cuts losses and lets winners run is the one instrument that can collect it.

Sources: sector and cross-asset funds, Alpha Vantage daily closes, split-adjusted, distributions excluded. Index levels, ^GSPC and ^SP500TR. Macro series from FRED. Positioning from the CFTC Traders in Financial Futures report, keyed on contract market code. Auction results from the U.S. Treasury Fiscal Data service, 7,841 auctions from January 2000. Macro-overlay figures are independently reconstructed from the same FRED series and cross-checked against the regime study's published tables.
